activities.\n  manifestation: I’m constantly hunting for things I need to work, like my USB-C adapters, stylus, sketchbook, or even my badge, and it can derail a whole morning because I have no memory If I’m sitting there too long, I feel this pressure building up to move, so I’ll start shifting around or switch chairs. Sometimes I just get up and pace around my office while I’m still trying to look like I’m paying attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness at home or when you're not working, as well?\n\nPatient: Yes, it happens at home too. After I’ve worked late, I’ll try to sit quietly or read something non-work-related to wind down, but after a few minutes I’m tapping my fingers, checking my phone, or getting up to adjust something around the room. I don’t really settle into it.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention or sitting still when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, looking back, it was there in grade school. Teachers would say I was bright, but I daydreamed a lot. I’d finish assignments and then forget to turn them in, or I’d lose my homework folder. That was happening well before I was a teenager.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking or blurt out answers before they've finished asking a question?\n\nPatient: I don’t think that’s a big issue for me. I mean, I might occasionally jump in if I’m excited or think I know where someone’s going, but I haven’t really noticed myself constantly interrupting people or blurting things out.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focus, restlessness, or organization ever caused problems in your relationships or at work?\n\nPatient: Yes. Work is where it’s become the most obvious. It’s started to hurt how people see me there. I missed a checkpoint for a patent filing, which was a big deal, and after a tough architecture review, some of the people I work with seemed to question whether I could really lead the larger project. That was hard to hear, but I understand why they’d be concerned.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus and restlessness consistently throughout your adult life?\n\nPatient: Not really consistently my whole adult life, no. I mean, I’ve had some tendencies before, but it became a real problem about a year or so ago—maybe a year and a month—after that award pushed me into leading more coordination-heavy work. Since then it’s been much harder to manage.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I’m having a hard time keeping all the moving parts organized. I’ve got patent notes, workshop prep, gallery deadlines, team decisions… and instead of doing what actually matters, I end up just starting with whatever feels loudest or most urgent in the moment. It’s gotten to the point where I don’t feel like I can manage it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having trouble focusing on a single task for an extended period of time?\n\nPatient: Yes. If I’m coding and something interrupts me, even something small like a Slack ping or a tab flashing, I can completely lose the thread. Sometimes just a visual idea pops into my head and I’ll end up going down some unrelated design rabbit hole for twenty minutes before I realize I’m not doing the original task anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed when you're having trouble focusing or getting interrupted?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious exactly. It’s more that I get frustrated and kind of overloaded when I can’t get back to what I was doing. Like I know there was a thread there, but I can’t grab it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Mostly on long design calls. If I’m sitting there too long, I feel this pressure building up to move, so I’ll start shifting around or switch chairs. Sometimes I just get up and pace around my office while I’m still trying to look like I’m paying attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness at home or when you're not working, as well?\n\nPatient: Yes, it happens at home too. After I’ve worked late, I’ll try to sit quietly or read something non-work-related to wind down, but after a few minutes I’m tapping my fingers, checking my phone, or getting up to adjust something around the room. I don’t really settle into it.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention or sitting still when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, looking back, it was there in grade school. Teachers would say I was bright, but I daydreamed a lot. I’d finish assignments and then forget to turn them in, or I’d lose my homework folder. That was happening well before I was a teenager.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking or blurt out answers before they've finished asking a question?\n\nPatient: I don’t think that’s a big issue for me. I mean, I might occasionally jump in if I’m excited or think I know where someone’s going, but I haven’t really noticed myself constantly interrupting people or blurting things out.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focus, restlessness, or organization ever caused problems in your relationships or at work?\n\nPatient: Yes. Work is where it’s become the most obvious. It’s started to hurt how people see me there. I missed a checkpoint for a patent filing, which was a big deal, and after a tough architecture review, some of the people I work with seemed to question whether I could really lead the larger project. That was hard to hear, but I understand why they’d be concerned.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus and restlessness consistently throughout your adult life?\n\nPatient: Not really consistently my whole adult life, no. I mean, I’ve had some tendencies before, but it became a real problem about a year or so ago—maybe a year and a month—after that award pushed me into leading more coordination-heavy work. Since then it’s been much harder to manage.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced periods where your symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity were absent or much less noticeable?\n\nPatient: Yes, before about a year ago it was much less noticeable. It’s really been a problem for about a year and a month now, after that award led to me taking on more coordination-heavy work. Since then, it’s been harder to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your difficulties with focus, restlessness, and organization are causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I’m pretty worried about what it’s doing to my reputation at work. Missing that patent filing checkpoint was embarrassing and had real consequences, and after the architecture review, I could tell some collaborators were questioning whether I’m able to lead the bigger project. That’s been weighing on me a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness when you're on vacation or completely away from work and other responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t paid close attention to it when I’m fully away from work. On vacation I’m usually not tracking it the same way, so I can’t say clearly whether it’s still there or not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus and restlessness happen when you're doing things you enjoy, like hobbies?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Even when I’m trying to do something I want to enjoy, like reading something nontechnical after working late, I don’t really settle into it. After a few minutes I’ll be tapping, checking my phone, or getting up to adjust something instead of actually winding down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus and restlessness mostly happen in situations where there's a lot of structure or expectation, like at work, or can they also occur in more unstructured or relaxed settings?\n\nPatient: It can happen in relaxed settings too. Like after working late, I’ll try to just sit quietly or read something nontechnical, and within a few minutes I’m tapping, checking my phone, or getting up to adjust something. So it’s not only when there’s a work deadline or a lot of structure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I’m having a hard time keeping all the moving parts organized. I’ve got patent notes, workshop prep, gallery deadlines, team decisions… and instead of doing what actually matters, I end up just starting with whatever feels loudest or most urgent in the moment. It’s gotten to the point where I don’t feel like I can manage it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having trouble focusing on a single task for an extended period of time?\n\nPatient: Yes. If I’m coding and something interrupts me, even something small like a Slack ping or a tab flashing, I can completely lose the thread. Sometimes just a visual idea pops into my head and I’ll end up going down some unrelated design rabbit hole for twenty minutes before I realize I’m not doing the original task anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed when you're having trouble focusing or getting interrupted?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious exactly. It’s more that I get frustrated and kind of overloaded when I can’t get back to what I was doing. Like I know there was a thread there, but I can’t grab it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Mostly on long design calls. If I’m sitting there too long, I feel this pressure building up to move, so I’ll start shifting around or switch chairs. Sometimes I just get up and pace around my office while I’m still trying to look like I’m paying attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness at home or when you're not working, as well?\n\nPatient: Yes, it happens at home too. After I’ve worked late, I’ll try to sit quietly or read something non-work-related to wind down, but after a few minutes I’m tapping my fingers, checking my phone, or getting up to adjust something around the room. I don’t really settle into it.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention or sitting still when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, looking back, it was there in grade school. Teachers would say I was bright, but I daydreamed a lot. I’d finish assignments and then forget to turn them in, or I’d lose my homework folder. That was happening well before I was a teenager.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking or blurt out answers before they've finished asking a question?\n\nPatient: I don’t think that’s a big issue for me. I mean, I might occasionally jump in if I’m excited or think I know where someone’s going, but I haven’t really noticed myself constantly interrupting people or blurting things out.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focus, restlessness, or organization ever caused problems in your relationships or at work?\n\nPatient: Yes. Work is where it’s become the most obvious. It’s started to hurt how people see me there. I missed a checkpoint for a patent filing, which was a big deal, and after a tough architecture review, some of the people I work with seemed to question whether I could really lead the larger project. That was hard to hear, but I understand why they’d be concerned.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus and restlessness consistently throughout your adult life?\n\nPatient: Not really consistently my whole adult life, no. I mean, I’ve had some tendencies before, but it became a real problem about a year or so ago—maybe a year and a month—after that award pushed me into leading more coordination-heavy work. Since then it’s been much harder to manage.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced periods where your symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity were absent or much less noticeable?\n\nPatient: Yes, before about a year ago it was much less noticeable. It’s really been a problem for about a year and a month now, after that award led to me taking on more coordination-heavy work. Since then, it’s been harder to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your difficulties with focus, restlessness, and organization are causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I’m pretty worried about what it’s doing to my reputation at work. Missing that patent filing checkpoint was embarrassing and had real consequences, and after the architecture review, I could tell some collaborators were questioning whether I’m able to lead the bigger project. That’s been weighing on me a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness when you're on vacation or completely away from work and other responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t paid close attention to it when I’m fully away from work. On vacation I’m usually not tracking it the same way, so I can’t say clearly whether it’s still there or not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus and restlessness happen when you're doing things you enjoy, like hobbies?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Even when I’m trying to do something I want to enjoy, like reading something nontechnical after working late, I don’t really settle into it. After a few minutes I’ll be tapping, checking my phone, or getting up to adjust something instead of actually winding down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus and restlessness mostly happen in situations where there's a lot of structure or expectation, like at work, or can they also occur in more unstructured or relaxed settings?\n\nPatient: It can happen in relaxed settings too. Like after working late, I’ll try to just sit quietly or read something nontechnical, and within a few minutes I’m tapping, checking my phone, or getting up to adjust something. So it’s not only when there’s a work deadline or a lot of structure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus and restlessness would improve if your work demands and responsibilities were significantly reduced?\n\nPatient: Maybe somewhat, but I don’t think it would fully go away. What really pushed me to come in is that I can’t organize all the moving parts anymore—patent notes, workshop prep, gallery deadlines, team decisions. Even if the workload were lighter, I feel like I’d still end up jumping to whatever feels most urgent or loudest instead of what actually matters.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s gotten to the point where I don’t feel like I can manage it the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having trouble focusing on a single task for an extended period of time?\n\nPatient: Yes. If I’m coding and something interrupts me, even something small like a Slack ping or a tab flashing, I can completely lose the thread. Sometimes just a visual idea pops into my head and I’ll end up going down some unrelated design rabbit hole for twenty minutes before I realize I’m not doing the original task anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed when you're having trouble focusing or getting interrupted?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious exactly. It’s more that I get frustrated and kind of overloaded when I can’t get back to what I was doing. Like I know there was a thread there, but I can’t grab it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Mostly on long design calls. If I’m sitting there too long, I feel this pressure building up to move, so I’ll start shifting around or switch chairs. Sometimes I just get up and pace around my office while I’m still trying to look like I’m paying attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness at home or when you're not working, as well?\n\nPatient: Yes, it happens at home too. After I’ve worked late, I’ll try to sit quietly or read something non-work-related to wind down, but after a few minutes I’m tapping my fingers, checking my phone, or getting up to adjust something around the room. I don’t really settle into it.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention or sitting still when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, looking back, it was there in grade school. Teachers would say I was bright, but I daydreamed a lot. I’d finish assignments and then forget to turn them in, or I’d lose my homework folder. That was happening well before I was a teenager.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking or blurt out answers before they've finished asking a question?\n\nPatient: I don’t think that’s a big issue for me. I mean, I might occasionally jump in if I’m excited or think I know where someone’s going, but I haven’t really noticed myself constantly interrupting people or blurting things out.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focus, restlessness, or organization ever caused problems in your relationships or at work?\n\nPatient: Yes. Work is where it’s become the most obvious. It’s started to hurt how people see me there. I missed a checkpoint for a patent filing, which was a big deal, and after a tough architecture review, some of the people I work with seemed to question whether I could really lead the larger project. That was hard to hear, but I understand why they’d be concerned.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus and restlessness consistently throughout your adult life?\n\nPatient: Not really consistently my whole adult life, no. Since then, it’s been harder to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your difficulties with focus, restlessness, and organization are causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I’m pretty worried about what it’s doing to my reputation at work. That’s been weighing on me a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness when you're on vacation or completely away from work and other responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t paid close attention to it when I’m fully away from work. After a few minutes I’ll be tapping, checking my phone, or getting up to adjust something instead of actually winding down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus and restlessness mostly happen in situations where there's a lot of structure or expectation, like at work, or can they also occur in more unstructured or relaxed settings?\n\nPatient: It can happen in relaxed settings too. So it’s not only when there’s a work deadline or a lot of structure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus and restlessness would improve if your work demands and responsibilities were significantly reduced?\n\nPatient: Maybe somewhat, but I don’t think it would fully go away. What really pushed me to come in is that I can’t organize all the moving parts anymore—patent notes, workshop prep, gallery deadlines, team decisions. Even if the workload were lighter, I feel like I’d still end up jumping to whatever feels most urgent or loudest instead of what actually matters.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant difficulties with focus, restlessness, or organization for at least 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yes. Sometimes I just get up and pace around my office while I’m still trying to look like I’m paying attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness at home or when you're not working, as well?\n\nPatient: Yes, it happens at home too. After I’ve worked late, I’ll try to sit quietly or read something non-work-related to wind down, but after a few minutes I’m tapping my fingers, checking my phone, or getting up to adjust something around the room. I don’t really settle into it.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble paying attention or sitting still when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, looking back, it was there in grade school. Teachers would say I was bright, but I daydreamed a lot. I’d finish assignments and then forget to turn them in, or I’d lose my homework folder. That was happening well before I was a teenager.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking or blurt out answers before they've finished asking a question?\n\nPatient: I don’t think that’s a big issue for me. I mean, I might occasionally jump in if I’m excited or think I know where someone’s going, but I haven’t really noticed myself constantly interrupting people or blurting things out.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focus, restlessness, or organization ever caused problems in your relationships or at work?\n\nPatient: Yes. Work is where it’s become the most obvious. It’s started to hurt how people see me there. I missed a checkpoint for a patent filing, which was a big deal, and after a tough architecture review, some of the people I work with seemed to question whether I could really lead the larger project. That was hard to hear, but I understand why they’d be concerned.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus and restlessness consistently throughout your adult life?\n\nPatient: Not really consistently my whole adult life, no. I mean, I’ve had some tendencies before, but it became a real problem about a year or so ago—maybe a year and a month—after that award pushed me into leading more coordination-heavy work. Since then it’s been much harder to manage.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced periods where your symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity were absent or much less noticeable?\n\nPatient: Yes, before about a year ago it was much less noticeable. It’s really been a problem for about a year and a month now, after that award led to me taking on more coordination-heavy work. Since then, it’s been harder to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your difficulties with focus, restlessness, and organization are causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I’m pretty worried about what it’s doing to my reputation at work. Missing that patent filing checkpoint was embarrassing and had real consequences, and after the architecture review, I could tell some collaborators were questioning whether I’m able to lead the bigger project. That’s been weighing on me a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness when you're on vacation or completely away from work and other responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t paid close attention to it when I’m fully away from work. On vacation I’m usually not tracking it the same way, so I can’t say clearly whether it’s still there or not.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus and restlessness happen when you're doing things you enjoy, like hobbies?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Even when I’m trying to do something I want to enjoy, like reading something nontechnical after working late, I don’t really settle into it. After a few minutes I’ll be tapping, checking my phone, or getting up to adjust something instead of actually winding down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus and restlessness mostly happen in situations where there's a lot of structure or expectation, like at work, or can they also occur in more unstructured or relaxed settings?\n\nPatient: It can happen in relaxed settings too. Like after working late, I’ll try to just sit quietly or read something nontechnical, and within a few minutes I’m tapping, checking my phone, or getting up to adjust something. So it’s not only when there’s a work deadline or a lot of structure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus and restlessness would improve if your work demands and responsibilities were significantly reduced?\n\nPatient: Maybe somewhat, but I don’t think it would fully go away. What really pushed me to come in is that I can’t organize all the moving parts anymore—patent notes, workshop prep, gallery deadlines, team decisions. Even if the workload were lighter, I feel like I’d still end up jumping to whatever feels most urgent or loudest instead of what actually matters.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant difficulties with focus, restlessness, or organization for at least 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s been more than six months — about a year and a month now. It really became a problem after the award pushed me into leading more coordination-heavy work. Since then the focus and organization issues have been pretty consistent.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, or impulsivity been present for most days over the past year?\n\nPatient: Yes, most days. It’s been a real problem for about a year and a month now, since that award pushed me into more coordination-heavy work. It hasn’t really just come and gone — it’s been pretty steady.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
